=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a length-N sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N).
You will repeatedly perform the following operation until the sequence has length at most 1: choose two adjacent numbers and remove both from the sequence.
The score obtained in one operation is the absolute difference of the two chosen numbers.
Find the maximum possible total score obtained.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the maximum possible total score obtained.

Constraints


- 2 \le N \le 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \le A_i \le 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
1 2 5 3

Sample Output 1

5

First, remove A_2 and A_3. The score obtained is |A_2 - A_3| = 3.
Next, remove A_1 and A_4. Note that, because of the previous operation, these two numbers are now adjacent. The score obtained is |A_1 - A_4| = 2.
Hence, the total score obtained is 5.
It is impossible to achieve a total score of 6 or greater, so print 5.

Sample Input 2

7
3 1 4 1 5 9 2

Sample Output 2

14

Sample Input 3

5
1 1 1 1 1

Sample Output 3

0

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

But maybe easier: Compute G for all even-length prefixes using a data structure that supports adding elements and querying sum top half - bottom half. Since each prefix length increases by 2, we can add two elements and update.

How to maintain lower half and upper half sums for a multiset of even size 2t, with lower half = t smallest, upper half = t largest. We can use two heaps:
- max-heap for lower half (store negatives) with size t.
- min-heap for upper half with size t.
Invariant: all elements in lower <= all in upper, sizes equal.
When adding a new element x:
  If lower is empty or x <= -lower[0]? Actually lower max. Add to lower, then rebalance: if lower size > upper size +1? But we add two at a time; easier add one by one and maintain sizes equal after each pair? Since we only query after even length, but adding two sequentially can temporarily have odd size. We can maintain after each addition with sizes differing at most 1 and lower <= upper. But for G after even length, sizes equal. Need handle two additions.

Alternative: Use a sorted list? N 3e5, O(N sqrt) not.

Could use two heaps with rebalancing after each element. Standard median maintenance:
- lower max-heap, upper min-heap.
- Add x: if lower empty or x <= -lower[0], push to lower; else push to upper.
- Rebalance: if len(lower) > len(upper)+1: move max from lower to upper. If len(upper) > len(lower): move min from upper to lower. This maintains lower size = ceil(total/2), upper size = floor(total/2), and all lower <= upper.
After even total 2t, lower size = t, upper size = t. G = sum(upper) - sum(lower). But wait if total even, lower and upper equal size. Good.

Need maintain sums of heaps. Python heapq is min-heap; lower as negative values. sum_lower = sum of actual lower elements. We can update.

When adding two elements, after both additions total even, lower=upper=t. G = sum_upper - sum_lower. But note if duplicates and equal, invariant all lower <= upper; fine.

Let's test with [1,2,100] prefix length2: add1 lower [1], upper empty. add2: 2 > lower max1, push upper [2]. sizes lower1 upper1. G=2-1=1. Good.

Let's ensure the heap function returns pref list with zeros at odd indices? We only access even indices. We can allocate list of length n+1 zeros. For odd n, compute pref[2], pref[4], ..., pref[n-1]. For even n, compute pref[2],...,pref[n]. We'll write:

def compute_prefix_gaps(a):
    n = len(a)
    pref = [0]*(n+1)
    lower = []  # max heap for lower half, store negative
    upper = []  # min heap for upper half
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0

    def add(x):
        nonlocal sum_low, sum_high
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heapq.heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x
        # rebalance
        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heapq.heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

    # add pairs
    limit = n if n % 2 == 0 else n-1
    for length in range(2, limit+1, 2):
        add(a[length-2])
        add(a[length-1])
        pref[length] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

But note for n odd, limit=n-1, range(2,n,2) works. For n even, limit=n, range(2,n+1,2). Good.

Potential issue: In `add`, after pushing to lower, if len(lower) > len(upper)+1, move max to upper. But what if len(upper) > len(lower) after moving? Shouldn't happen. Standard.

But wait, if x is pushed to lower and lower size becomes upper+2, we move one. If x pushed to upper and upper size becomes lower+1, we move one from upper to lower. But what if after moving from lower to upper, upper size becomes lower+1? Example lower size2 upper0, push to lower -> lower3 upper0, move one -> lower2 upper1, okay. If lower size1 upper1, push to lower -> lower2 upper1, no move (2 > 2 false). If push to upper -> lower1 upper2, move one -> lower2 upper1. Good.

Actually if x > lower max, we push to upper. It may be smaller than current upper min, e.g., lower [1,2], upper [10], x=5 >2 push upper [5,10], then upper>lower? sizes lower2 upper2? The uncrossing property for absolute differences on a line with points ordered by value, not by position. For points on a line, if edges are crossing in geometric position order (which is also value order if points are on line sorted by coordinate), then uncrossing preserves sum. Now, answer loop:
ans = 0
for p in range(0, n, 2):

We used coloring by lower/upper half and adjacent opposite colors. Need be precise: Given a sequence of length 2t, color t positions as L (lower half values) and t as H (upper half values). We need find a noncrossing perfect matching where every pair is L-H. Does adjacent opposite color always exist? Suppose sequence has equal L and H. If no adjacent opposite colors, then all adjacent pairs have same color, so the whole sequence is one color, contradiction unless t=0. Thus there is at least one adjacent opposite pair. Remove them. The remaining sequence still has equal L and H (one each removed). Repeat. This yields a sequence of removals of adjacent pairs, each pair L-H. The score sum = sum H values - sum L values = G. This is exactly an operation sequence within the interval (removing adjacent pairs). Nice! This is even stronger: we don't need arbitrary matching. Great.

For even N whole, same.

For odd N with survivor p, if we choose p, left and right intervals each have even length. We can color each interval's lower/upper half and remove adjacent opposite pairs independently. The survivor remains. The total score is sum G. Upper bound as above. So algorithm is solid.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the survivor position parity in the coloring proof? We choose p even 0-based. Left and right lengths even. If p odd 0-based, lengths odd, impossible. Our loop only even p. Good.

Now, let's consider if the operation sequence for left and right intervals can be interleaved arbitrarily; yes, they are separated by survivor, so removing pairs in left doesn't affect adjacency in right. We can perform all left removals then all right removals or interleave. The survivor remains.

Now, let's think about computing G for prefixes and suffixes using heaps in terms of lower/upper halves. The heap maintainer after adding elements in pairs gives G for the multiset of all added elements. This is exactly G(prefix length). For reversed, G(suffix). Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute prefix gaps with a simpler method using two heaps but adding elements one by one and storing after even lengths. Our loop adds pairs. Need ensure when adding two elements, after first addition the heap sizes may be lower=upper+1 or upper=lower? After second, equal. The `add` function handles odd intermediate. Good.

Now, let's think about possible pitfalls in heap maintainer when adding elements in pairs and computing pref. We store pref[length] after adding both elements. But what about if `add` function rebalances and moves elements between heaps, the sums update correctly. Yes.

Let's write code:

import sys, heapq

def compute_prefix_gaps(a):
    n = len(a)
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []  # max heap (store negative)
    upper = []  # min heap
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0

    def add(x):
        nonlocal sum_low, sum_high
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heapq.heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x

        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heapq.heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

    limit = n if n % 2 == 0 else n - 1
    for length in range(2, limit + 1, 2):
        add(a[length - 2])
        add(a[length - 1])
        pref[length] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    if N % 2 == 0:
        pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A)
        print(pref[N])
    else:
        pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A)
        rev = compute_prefix_gaps(A[::-1])
        ans = 0
        for p in range(0, N, 2):  # 0-based survivor index
            val = pref[p] + rev[N - p - 1]
            if val > ans:
                ans = val
        print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

- Add second element: if x <= lower max, push lower -> sizes2,0, rebalance lower>upper+1 (2>1) move one to upper -> sizes1,1. If x > lower max, push upper -> sizes1,1. So after pair sizes equal.
- Inductively after even total sizes equal. After first of next pair sizes differ by1; after second equal. Good.

Code:

import sys, heapq

def compute_prefix_gaps(a):
    n = len(a)
    pref = [0]*(n+1)
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0

    def add(x):
        nonlocal sum_low, sum_high
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heapq.heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x

        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heapq.heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

    limit = n if n % 2 == 0 else n - 1
    for length in range(2, limit + 1, 2):
        add(a[length-2])
        add(a[length-1])
        pref[length] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    if N % 2 == 0:
        pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A)
        print(pref[N])
    else:
        pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A)
        rev = compute_prefix_gaps(A[::-1])
        ans = 0
        for p in range(0, N, 2):
            val = pref[p] + rev[N - p - 1]
            if val > ans:
                ans = val
        print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think about if the score for a fixed p can be achieved by adjacent opposite color removal within each side, but the color assignment to positions depends on lower/upper halves of that side. We can choose any assignment of equal values. The adjacent opposite color removal algorithm pairs adjacent positions with different colors. But what if two adjacent positions have same color and no opposite adjacent? We argued if equal counts and length>0, there is at least one adjacent opposite. Let's prove: Suppose no adjacent opposite colors. Then for every i, color_i = color_{i+1}. By transitivity all colors same. Contradiction. So yes. Remove one adjacent opposite pair; counts remain equal. Repeat. This yields a sequence of adjacent removals within the interval. The score of each removed pair is |value_H - value_L|. Since H values are from upper half and L from lower half, the sum over all pairs = sum_H - sum_L = G. Note if a lower value equals an upper value, diff0, still okay. The adjacent opposite removal may pair a lower value with an upper value but not necessarily the sorted extremes; total still G because sum of all H - sum of all L. Good.

Now, let's think about if the upper bound G(interval) for any matching within interval holds even if a pair has equal values and classification arbitrary. For any pair, contribution = larger - smaller. Let H be the set of larger elements in each pair. Since there are t pairs, H has t elements. The sum of H is at most the sum of the t largest values in the interval. Similarly L sum at least sum of t smallest. Thus total <= G. This is true regardless of matching. Good.

Let's rewrite `compute_prefix_gaps` using this approach:

def compute_prefix_gaps(a, limit):
    # limit is number of elements to consider, even
    pref = [0]*(len(a)+1)  # but if a is full length, pref length n+1; if a is A[:-1]? We can pass full A and limit.
    lower=[]; upper=[]; sum_low=0; sum_high=0
    for i in range(limit):
        x = a[i]
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heapq.heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x
        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heapq.heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v
        if i % 2 == 1:
            pref[i+1] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

Now, let's rewrite code with this function:

def compute_prefix_gaps(a, limit):
    n = len(a)
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0
    for i in range(limit):
        x = a[i]
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heapq.heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x
        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heapq.heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v
        if i & 1:
            pref[i + 1] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

Code:

def compute_prefix_gaps(a, limit):
    n = len(a)
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0
    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop
    for i in range(limit):
        x = a[i]
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x
        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v
        if i & 1:
            pref[i + 1] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

if N % 2 == 0:
    pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A, N)
    print(pref[N])
else:
    pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A, N - 1)
    rev = compute_prefix_gaps(A[::-1], N - 1)
    ans = 0
    for p in range(0, N, 2):
        val = pref[p] + rev[N - p - 1]
        if val > ans:
            ans = val
    print(ans)

Now, let's revisit the proof of G upper bound for even interval with lower/upper halves. For a matching, each pair contributes max-min. Let H be the larger elements. There are t H elements. The sum of H is at most sum of t largest values because H is a t-element subset of the interval. The sum of L (smaller elements) is at least sum of t smallest values because L is a t-element subset. Thus total <= sum largest t - sum smallest t. This is a standard rearrangement. Good.

Now, let's think about if the adjacent opposite color removal achieves exactly G even if the lower/upper color assignment is arbitrary among equal boundary values. Suppose interval values [1,2,2,3], lower half could be {1,2}, upper {2,3}. G= (2+3)-(1+2)=2. If we color one of the 2s as lower and the other as upper, any L-H pair sum of H-L over all pairs = (sum upper values) - (sum lower values) =2 regardless of which 2 is colored? If the two 2s are equal, sum upper =2+3=5, sum lower=1+2=3, diff2. If we swapped, same. Good. If there are multiple equal boundary values, the sum difference is fixed. So any assignment works.

Now, let's think about if the adjacent opposite color removal algorithm might pair a lower value with an upper value where lower > upper due to equal boundary? No, by definition lower half values <= upper half values. If we assign equal boundary values, still lower <= upper. Good.

Now, let's consider if for odd N, the left and right intervals' lower/upper halves are independent; the survivor value doesn't matter. Good.

The wording: "You will repeatedly perform the following operation until the sequence has length at most 1" means you continue while length >1. So cannot stop at length2. Good.

def compute_prefix_gaps(values, n, limit):
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0
    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop
    for i, x in enumerate(values):
        if i >= limit:
            break
        ...

    return pref

Then in main:

    print(pref[N])
else:
    pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A, N, N - 1)
    rev = compute_prefix_gaps(reversed(A), N, N - 1)
    ans = max(pref[p] + rev[N - p - 1] for p in range(0, N, 2))
    print(ans)

Function:

def compute_prefix_gaps(values, n, limit):
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0
    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop
    it = iter(values)
    for i in range(limit):
        x = next(it)
        ...
        if i & 1:
            pref[i + 1] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

We can compute prefix gaps for original and reversed in one pass? Not easily. But two passes of 300k each is okay. Let's approximate: 600k iterations, each iteration does a heappush. heappush in Python is implemented in Python? Actually `heapq` is a Python module with functions written in Python. 600k * log2(150k) ~ 600k*18 =10.8M loop iterations inside heapq, plus Python overhead. 10.8M is okay (<1s? maybe 1-2s). Rebalance heappop adds similar. Worst-case each add may trigger a heappop, so another 10.8M. Total ~20M operations, PyPy maybe 2s. AtCoder time limit maybe 2 sec but PyPy can handle. If concerned, we can use sorting for even and maybe use a different O(N log N) with `heapq` but fewer operations? No.

Could we compute prefix G using a Fenwick tree over compressed values with O(N log N) but each update/query O(log N) and no heappop? Similar.

Could we compute G for all prefixes using a "two heaps" but adding two elements at a time and rebalancing once per pair instead of per element? That would reduce rebalance operations. Let's explore.

We can add two elements x,y to the current lower/upper heaps of equal size t. We need final lower/upper size t+1. We can insert both elements into appropriate heaps based on comparison with current lower max and upper min, then rebalance to equal sizes. This would involve at most two pushes and maybe two pops per pair, same as one-by-one but maybe less overhead. One-by-one is fine.

Could we compute prefix G using `heapq.merge`? No.

Let's think if there is an O(N) algorithm using the fact that we only need G for prefixes of a sequence, not arbitrary online? Maybe we can compute G for all prefixes by maintaining two heaps; that's standard. O(N log N) is intended.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code if the problem's memory limit is 256MB; our memory okay. Let's estimate with reversed iterator (no A[::-1]):
- data list: after `data = list(map(...))`, data has N+1 ints. A = data[1:] creates a new list of N references. del data frees original list of N+1 references but int objects remain referenced by A. So memory: A list 2.4MB + int objects 300k*28=8.4MB =10.8MB.
- pref list: length N+1, initially all references to 0; after assignments, even indices (150k) reference new ints. List pointers 2.4MB + ints 4.2MB =6.6MB.
- rev list: another 6.6MB.
- Heaps during compute: lower/upper contain up to N-1 references. For lower, negative ints created; for upper, references to existing ints from A? Let's draft final code:

import sys
import heapq

def compute_prefix_gaps(values, n, limit):
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0
    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop
    it = iter(values)
    for i in range(limit):
        x = next(it)
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x
        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v
        if i & 1:
            pref[i + 1] = sum_high - sum_low
    return pref

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    # del data
    if N % 2 == 0:
        s = sorted(A)
        ans = sum(s[N//2:]) - sum(s[:N//2])
        print(ans)
    else:
        pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A, N, N - 1)
        rev = compute_prefix_gaps(reversed(A), N, N - 1)
        ans = 0
        for p in range(0, N, 2):
            val = pref[p] + rev[N - p - 1]
            if val > ans:
                ans = val
        print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Should we `del data`? If we do `A = data[1:]`, data list remains until main ends. For memory, we can `del data` after A. But if we sort A, data list still references ints; deleting data frees list. Let's include `del data` after A. However, if we do `A = data[1:]`, then `del data` is safe. Let's include:
    A = data[1:]
    del data
This reduces memory. But if we sort A, A is a list of references to int objects; data deletion doesn't affect ints. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code if the problem's constraints include N=300000 and even branch, but `ans` is int; print. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code if the problem's constraints include N=300000 and odd branch, but `compute_prefix_gaps` uses `n` to allocate pref; if `values` is reversed, `
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import heapq


def compute_prefix_gaps(values, n, limit):
    pref = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []  # max heap, stored as negatives
    upper = []  # min heap
    sum_low = 0
    sum_high = 0

    heappush = heapq.heappush
    heappop = heapq.heappop

    it = iter(values)
    for i in range(limit):
        x = next(it)

        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heappush(upper, x)
            sum_high += x

        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heappush(upper, v)
            sum_high += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_high -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        if i & 1:
            pref[i + 1] = sum_high - sum_low

    return pref


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    del data

    if N % 2 == 0:
        A.sort()
        half = N // 2
        ans = sum(A) - 2 * sum(A[:half])
        print(ans)
    else:
        pref = compute_prefix_gaps(A, N, N - 1)
        rev = compute_prefix_gaps(reversed(A), N, N - 1)

        ans = 0
        for p in range(0, N, 2):  # survivor must be at an odd original position
            val = pref[p] + rev[N - p - 1]
            if val > ans:
                ans = val

        print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
